How does limma-trend work? An empirical partially Bayes perspective
Sagnik Nandy, Wanyi Ling, Nikolaos Ignatiadis

Abstract
In high-throughput biology, it is common to fit thousands of linear regressions—one per gene, protein, or other unit—with very few samples per unit. Limma-trend , one of the most widely used methods in this setting, improves power by shrinking variance estimates parametrically toward a fitted curve (the trend) relating variance to a unit-level summary (e.g., average intensity, peptide count), before computing p-values and applying the Benjamini-Hochberg procedure to control the false discovery rate (FDR). We study limma-trend through the lens of empirical partially Bayes inference, a paradigm in which a prior is posited and estimated for the nuisance parameters while parameters of interest remain fixed. From this perspective, limma-trend computes approximate partially Bayes p-values that condition on the residual sample variance and the unit-level summary. The same framework explains why MAnorm2, a popular variant for ChIP-seq, can sometimes fail to control FDR. We then derive a nonparametric generalization of limma-trend that estimates the residual variance prior using nonparametric maximum likelihood. Under dense signals, this procedure asymptotically controls the FDR—even when the trend is misspecified or inconsistently estimated. To allow the full shape of the conditional variance distribution to depend on the unit-level summary, we develop a second procedure that learns it directly.



1
Introduction

To this day, one of the most common data analysis tasks in high-throughput biology consists of fitting separate linear models for each of
n
units (e.g., genes, proteins) and performing statistical analysis using the outputs of these fits. For the
i
-th unit, the adopted statistical framework is standard:
K
samples,
p
covariates with
p<K
, homoscedastic Gaussian errors with the responses
Y_{ij}
modeled as

Y_{ij}=x_{j}^{\top}\beta_{i}+\varepsilon_{ij},\;\;\varepsilon_{ij}\,{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\sim}\limits^{\text{ind}}}\,}\mathrm{N}(0,\,\sigma_{i}^{2}),\;\;j=1,\dotsc,K,\;\;i=1,\dotsc,n.

(1)

The coefficients
\beta_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and noise variances
\sigma_{i}^{2}
are unknown, while the covariates
x_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
include, e.g., the intercept, treatment status, as well as confounding (batch effect) variables to adjust for. The interest of the practitioners typically centers on a single contrast
\theta_{i}=\beta_{i}^{\top}c_{\theta}
for pre-specified
c_{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
(e.g., the treatment effect). The statistical task is to test the hypotheses
H_{i}:\theta_{i}=0
for
i=1,\dots,n
. The number of units
n
is in the thousands, and the degrees of freedom
K-p
is often minuscule, sometimes in the single digits.

The textbook solution to this problem is immediate: for each unit
i
, compute the ordinary least squares estimate
\widehat{\theta}_{i}
and apply a
t
-test, followed by multiple testing correction. The relevant question is:
Does this allow us to make a significant amount of discoveries while controlling the false discoveries?
In genomics, small sample sizes (small
K
) severely limit the power of standard approaches; after multiple testing corrections, naïve
t
-tests often yield few or no discoveries. The
limma
framework
(
46
)
addresses this by shrinking variance estimates toward a common value before conducting inference, effectively borrowing strength across units. The
limma-trend
variant
(
28
)
, building on ideas of
45
, allows the shrinkage target to depend on unit-level summaries such as average intensity (defined below in (
3
)). This refinement has proven important:
limma-trend
underlies methods for RNA-seq
(
40
)
, proteomics
(
57
;
36
)
, ChIP-seq
(
51
)
, methylation variability
(
39
)
, cytometry
(
54
)
, lipidomics
(
50
)
, and other modalities.

Despite its widespread adoption,
limma-trend
has received relatively little formal statistical attention.
22
recently provided theoretical foundations for the original (untrended)
limma
procedure. In the trend setting, the unit-level summary toward which variances are shrunk is typically the average intensity, and the average intensity is itself computed from the same outcomes used for inference. This opens up three issues that have no analog in the untrended case: (i) the validity of the partially Bayes interpretation requires an orthogonality condition on the design that is not automatic, and is violated in the popular ChIP-seq package MAnorm2, (we show in simulations that this leads to an inflation in the FDR); (ii) the prior on variances is no longer a single shared distribution across units, but must vary with the summary; and (iii) the analysis must accommodate the plug-in of an estimated trend into the marginal likelihood used to learn that prior. In this paper, we develop a partially Bayes
(
9
)
account of
limma-trend
that handles all three issues, and we propose two nonparametric procedures motivated by this account.

1.1
Limma-trend
and our contributions

(a) RNA-Seq

(b) ChIP-Seq

(c) Proteomics

Figure 1
:
Mean-variance trend observed in datasets from three different biological modalities. Grey points represent the pair, unit-wise side-information (average intensity for (a) and (b),
\log_{2}
peptide counts for (c)), and log sample variance. Their relation is represented by two lines: a fitted natural cubic spline curve, and a constant trend.
(a)
RNA-Seq: each unit
i
represents a gene in
the CD4
+
T cells melanoma dataset from
20
.
(b)
ChIP-Seq: each unit
i
represents a genomic interval in the H3K4me3 lymphoblastoid cell dataset from
51
.
(c)
Proteomics: each unit
i
represents a protein in the miRNA-mimic treatment dataset on A431 cells from
57
.

In
limma-trend
, the practitioner begins by computing
the ordinary least squares estimates of
\beta_{i}
in (
1
), the primary contrast of interest
\theta_{i}
and the residual variance
\sigma_{i}^{2}
(for all units),

\widehat{\beta}_{i}:=(X^{\top}X)^{-1}X^{\top}Y_{i},\;\;\;\;Z_{i}:=\widehat{\beta}_{i}^{\top}c_{\theta},\;\;\;\;S^{2}_{i}:=\frac{1}{K-p}\sum_{j=1}^{K}(Y_{ij}-x_{j}^{\top}\widehat{\beta}_{i})^{2},\qquad\mbox{for $i=1,\ldots,n$,}

(2)

where
X\in\mathbb{R}^{K\times p}
denotes the design matrix (assumed to be full rank) with
j
-th row equal to
x_{j}^{\top}
and
Y_{i}:=(Y_{i1},\ldots,Y_{iK})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
.
The textbook approach to compute a p-value for testing
\theta_{i}=0
uses the fact that
T_{i}:=Z_{i}/(\nu S_{i})\sim t_{K-p}
(the t-distribution with
K-p
degrees of freedom) under the null, where
\nu:=\{c_{\theta}^{\top}(X^{\top}X)^{-1}c_{\theta}\}^{1/2}
, giving
P_{i}^{\texttt{t}}:=2\,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{t_{K-p}}(|T_{i}|)
(the survival function of
t_{K-p}
).

These
t
-test p-values often lack power because
S_{i}^{2}
is a noisy estimate of
\sigma_{i}^{2}
, as reflected by the heavy tails of the
t
-distribution at small
K-p
. The key idea underlying
limma-trend
is that, in common high-throughput biology applications, there is contextual information
M_{i}
, distinct from
S_{i}^{2}
, that is highly predictive of
\sigma_{i}^{2}
. Figure
1
plots estimates of the conditional expectation
\mathbb{E}\left[\log(S_{i}^{2})\mid M_{i}\right]
across three datasets from different modalities (with different choices of
M_{i}
); we further revisit these datasets in Section
6
. We refer to the function
\mathbb{E}\left[\log(S_{i}^{2})\mid M_{i}=\cdot\right]
and estimates thereof as
trends
. The trends in Figure
1
are far from constant.

How should one use the trend? Suppose for a moment that the
\sigma_{i}^{2}
are a deterministic function of
M_{i}
,
\sigma_{i}^{2}=h^{2}(M_{i})
, and that we have a consistent estimate
\widehat{h}^{2}(M_{i})
thereof. Then we could report the p-values
2\Phi(-|Z_{i}|/\{\nu\widehat{h}(M_{i})\})
, where
\Phi
is the standard Gaussian CDF, sidestepping the heavy tails of the
t
-distribution. This idea is not new, e.g., it appears in the MAP (model-based analysis of proteomic data) procedure of
30
. In practice, however,
\sigma_{i}^{2}
is
not
determined by
M_{i}
: there is residual heterogeneity that no trend can absorb, and methods that ignore this heterogeneity can inflate false discoveries, as we confirm for MAP in simulations in Section
5
.

This brings us to
limma-trend
, which is summarized in Algorithm
1
below. Step 1 fits the trend
\widehat{\xi}^{2}(M_{i})
. Step 2 estimates the distribution (prior) of the residual heterogeneity
\sigma_{i}^{2}/\widehat{\xi}^{2}(M_{i})
by empirical Bayes. Step 3 then computes p-values that combine
Z_{i}
,
S_{i}^{2}
, the trend evaluated at
M_{i}
(
\widehat{\xi}^{2}(M_{i})
), and the prior estimated in Step 2; in particular
S_{i}^{2}
enters the test statistic rather than being discarded. Steps 2 and 3 embed a specific parametric model of residual heterogeneity.

Algorithm 1

limma-trend
(
Reg-Inv
\chi^{2}
)

1:

Fit the trend of
\log(S_{i}^{2})
vs.
M_{i}
(e.g., as described in Supplement
B.1
), call this trend
\widehat{m}(\cdot)
and its exponential
\widehat{\xi}^{2}(\cdot)=\exp(\widehat{m}(\cdot))
.

2:

Estimate
\widehat{\kappa}_{0}
and
\widehat{s}_{0}^{2}
in the model
\widehat{\xi}^{2}(M_{i})/\sigma_{i}^{2}\sim\chi^{2}_{\kappa_{0}}/(\kappa_{0}s_{0}^{2})
, where
\chi^{2}_{\kappa_{0}}
is the chi-squared distribution with
\kappa_{0}
degrees of freedom.

3:

Compute
\widetilde{S}_{i}^{2}:=\{(K-p)S_{i}^{2}+\widehat{\kappa}_{0}\widehat{s}_{0}^{2}\widehat{\xi}^{2}(M_{i})\}/\{(K-p)+\widehat{\kappa}_{0}\}
,
\widetilde{T}_{i}:=Z_{i}/\{\nu\widetilde{S}_{i}\}
and estimated p-values
\widehat{P}^{\texttt{ltrd}}_{i}=2\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{t_{(K-p)+\widehat{\kappa}_{0}}}(|\widetilde{T}_{i}|)
.

Our interpretation of
limma-trend
is that it computes p-values conditional on
S_{i}^{2}
and
M_{i}
. This conditioning is not possible under a purely frequentist analysis, but becomes possible when the nuisance parameters carry a prior distribution, with the primary parameters
\theta_{i}
kept fixed. Such an analysis is called
partially Bayes

(
9
)
, and we develop it for
limma-trend
in Section
2
.
1
1

1

Although our focus is on the partially Bayes interpretation of
limma-trend
, we also provide purely frequentist results, with all parameters fixed, following the framework of compound decision theory. These results are briefly reviewed in Section
7
and presented in detail in Supplement
M
.

An important wrinkle in applications is the choice of
M_{i}
. The default choice in
limma-trend
is,

A_{i}:=\frac{1}{K}\sum_{j=1}^{K}Y_{ij},

(3)

which is called the
average intensity
.
But
A_{i}
is computed from the same outcomes
Y_{i}
as
Z_{i}
and
S_{i}^{2}
, and the partially Bayes setup quietly assumes their conditional independence given
(\beta_{i},\sigma_{i}^{2})
. Section
2.3
shows that a simple orthogonality condition on the design and contrast (Assumption
1
, Proposition
2
) restores this independence, and verifies that it holds for the standard two-sample and treatment-effect designs. The condition has not, to our knowledge, been spelled out before, and its violation is not harmless: the MAnorm2
(
51
)
package for ChIP-seq replaces
A_{i}
with a quantity that fails it, and our simulations in Section
5
show that this violation can inflate the false discovery rate (FDR).

From the partially Bayes perspective, the
\mathrm{Inv}\chi^{2}
specification (in Step 2 of Algorithm
1
) is one choice rather than a necessity. Replacing it with a prior estimated by the nonparametric maximum likelihood estimator (NPMLE)
(
41
;
26
)
yields
Reg-NPMLE
(Algorithm
2
, Section
3
). The resulting empirical Bayes p-values approximate the partially Bayes p-values one would form if the prior were known, at a rate dominated by the trend-estimation error (Theorem
8
), and Benjamini-Hochberg
(
2
)
applied to them controls the FDR asymptotically (Theorem
12
). More notably, even if the fitted trend
\widehat{\xi}^{2}
is inconsistent for the true trend, the nonparametric prior on residual heterogeneity absorbs the error and FDR control is preserved (Theorem
15
). The prior on residual heterogeneity captures variation that no deterministic trend can absorb; the same flexibility also buffers errors in the fitted trend. Procedures like MAP, which treat
\sigma_{i}^{2}
as a deterministic function of
M_{i}
, do neither.

A more ambitious procedure,
Joint-NPMLE
(Algorithm
3
, Section
4
), foregoes direct trend-fitting. Rather than modeling residual heterogeneity around a fitted trend, it estimates the joint distribution of
\sigma_{i}^{2}
and the population mean intensity
\mu_{i}:=\mathbb{E}\left[A_{i}\mid\beta_{i}\right]
across units, using a bivariate NPMLE applied to
(S_{i}^{2},A_{i})
. An Eddington-Tweedie representation of the partially Bayes p-values (Theorem
18
) drives the analysis and yields a near-parametric convergence rate of the p-values (Theorem
20
), despite the non-parametric estimation of the bivariate prior; asymptotic FDR control again follows (Theorem
22
).

Section
5
confirms the theory in simulation.
Reg-NPMLE
and
Joint-NPMLE
retain FDR control across the regimes we consider. The trended methods also recover more discoveries than their untrended counterparts. Section
6
applies the methods to bulk RNA-seq, ChIP-seq, and proteomics datasets. The proteomics analysis motivates a discrete variant of
Joint-NPMLE
that fits a separate NPMLE within each peptide-count stratum, which we view as a methodological successor to DEqMS
(
57
)
, a
limma-trend
variant popular for proteomics analyses.

1.2
Related work: mean-variance modeling in genomics

Mean-variance modeling underlies several important methods in genomics. We provide an incomplete and simplified taxonomy here, to emphasize the main aspects of our contribution.

Several authors have focused on consistent estimation of mean-variance trends, e.g.,
6
;
53
;
16
;
33
;
29
. These works vary in their exact objective, e.g., estimating trends as functions of latent parameter (e.g., as a function of
\mu_{i}
) or observables, using nonparametric or parametric methods etc. A key message of these papers is that in general a naïve nonparametric regression of
\log(S_{i}^{2})
(or
S_{i}^{2}
) on
A_{i}
will not yield a consistent estimate of the true trend function and propose more elaborate methods.

Although most of the above papers are motivated by the testing problem we consider,
only
33
formally develop p-values that use the learned trend.
Specifically,
33
assume that (under the null),
\sigma_{i}^{2}=\zeta^{2}(\mu_{i})
for a function
\zeta^{2}
that can be estimated consistently as
\widehat{\zeta}^{2}
. They then propose to compute a p-value for
H_{i}:\theta_{i}=0
as
\beta+\sup_{\mu_{i}\in\mathcal{I}_{i}}2\Phi(-|Z_{i}|/\{\nu\widehat{\zeta}(\mu_{i})\})
, where
\mathcal{I}_{i}
is a preliminary
(1-\beta)
-confidence interval for
\mu_{i}
at some (small) level
\beta\in(0,1)
. A notable difference from our approach is that the trend (
\zeta
) is a function of an unobserved quantity
\mu_{i}
(rather than the observed
A_{i}
), which makes it harder to estimate. The approach is also more conservative (because of the supremum in the p-value definition).

Earlier, we mentioned the MAP method
(
30
)
as an example of an approach that uses the estimated trend to compute p-values, but that in general does not control FDR.
We note that the authors of MAP were aware of the lack of FDR control of their method and proposed a heuristic to further inflate variances. However, it is unclear whether this heuristic provides FDR control. This highlights the importance of a rigorous statistical analysis such as the one we provide for
limma-trend
, which avoids the need for ad hoc corrections.

Finally, we note that several other key statistical methods in genomics
rely on learning mean-variance (or dispersion) relationships, e.g.,
edgeR
(
34
;
8
)
, voom
(
28
)
, and DESeq2
(
31
)
. We hope that our investigation of
limma-trend
will motivate analogous statistical inquiries into these and related procedures.

2
Statistical modeling and partially Bayes testing

2.1
Background on untrended
limma

Before turning to
limma-trend
, we review the partially Bayes framework for untrended
limma

(
46
;
22
)
; the trended generalization in Section
2.2
builds on this setup. Under model (
1
), the summary statistics in (
2
) satisfy

(Z_{i},S^{2}_{i})\mid\beta_{i},\sigma_{i}^{2}\,\sim\mathrm{N}(\theta_{i},\,\nu^{2}\sigma_{i}^{2})\otimes\frac{\sigma_{i}^{2}}{K-p}\chi^{2}_{K-p}.

(4)

Limma
only operates on the summary statistics
(Z_{i},S_{i}^{2})
, and so effectively for each unit, there are only two unknown parameters,
\theta_{i}
(the parameter of interest) and
\sigma_{i}^{2}
(a nuisance parameter). The distinctive feature of
limma
is that it imposes a prior on the
nuisance parameter
\sigma_{i}^{2}
,

\frac{1}{\sigma_{i}^{2}}\sim\frac{\chi^{2}_{\kappa_{0}}}{\kappa_{0}s_{0}^{2}},\quad\text{for some $\kappa_{0},s_{0}^{2}>0$,}

(5)

but not on the primary parameter
\theta_{i}
, which is instead treated as fixed (as in a frequentist analysis).
9
and
35
use the terminology “partially Bayes” for such an analysis that imposes a prior on only the nuisance parameters.
The upshot is that integrating over (
5
), we get:

P_{i}^{\texttt{limma}}:=2\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{t_{(K-p)+\kappa_{0}}}(|\widetilde{T}_{i}|)\mid(S_{i}^{2},\theta_{i}=0)\sim\mathrm{Unif}[0,1],\text{ for }\,\widetilde{T}_{i}:=\frac{Z_{i}}{\nu\widetilde{S}_{i}},\,\widetilde{S}_{i}^{2}:=\frac{(K-p)S_{i}^{2}+\kappa_{0}s_{0}^{2}}{(K-p)+\kappa_{0}}.

(6)

In words, the limma p-value is computed by
replacing
S_{i}^{2}
by the “moderated” version
\widetilde{S}_{i}^{2}
that shrinks
S_{i}^{2}
toward
s_{0}^{2}
before studentizing and increasing the degrees of freedom from
(K-p)
to
(K-p)+\kappa_{0}
. The p-values remain uniformly distributed under the null (marginalizing over (
5
)), and improves power.
In practice, one estimates
\widehat{\kappa}_{0}
and
\widehat{s}_{0}^{2}
using empirical Bayes
(
42
;
14
)
from
S_{1}^{2},\ldots,S_{n}^{2}
, yielding estimated p-values
\widehat{P}_{i}^{\texttt{limma}}
. We interpret
\widehat{\kappa}_{0}
as the degrees of freedom gained by sharing information across units.

The p-values in (
6
) admit an equivalent representation
that will be central to our extension to
limma-trend
. Integrating
\sigma_{i}^{2}

against its posterior
\Pi(\,\cdot\mid S_{i}^{2})
under the prior
in (
5
) yields

P_{i}^{\texttt{limma}{}}=2\int\Phi(-|Z_{i}|/\{\nu\sigma_{i}\})\,\Pi(\mathrm{d}\sigma_{i}^{2}\mid S_{i}^{2});

(7)

see, e.g.,
22
. The advantage
of (
7
) over (
6
) is that it is
meaningful for any prior
G
on
\sigma_{i}^{2}
, including nonparametric
priors.
22
pursue this by taking

\sigma_{i}^{2}\,{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\sim}\limits^{\text{iid}}}\,}G
for an unknown
G
estimated by the
NPMLE
(
41
;
26
)
; also
see
32
. They show that the resulting estimated
p-values
\widehat{P}_{i}^{\texttt{limma}{}}
converge to the oracle p-values

P_{i}^{\texttt{limma}{}}
at a nearly parametric rate (up to log factors), and
that applying the Benjamini-Hochberg procedure to
\widehat{P}_{i}^{\texttt{limma}{}}

asymptotically controls the FDR.

An important property of the oracle p-values in (
7
)
is that they are conditionally uniform given
S_{i}^{2}
under the null:

P_{i}^{\texttt{limma}{}}\mid(\theta_{i}=0,S_{i}^{2})\sim\mathrm{Unif}[0,1]
.
The ordinary
t
-test p-value
P_{i}^{\texttt{t}}
does not satisfy this
conditional property: for null units whose
S_{i}^{2}

underestimates
\sigma_{i}^{2}
, the
t
-statistic is inflated

(
22
, Proposition 5)
. In a multiple testing
context, rejections from the
t
-test are thus enriched for units that by chance have unusually small
S_{i}^{2}
. This is an advantage of the partially Bayes specification over a purely frequentist analysis: it is the prior
G
on
\sigma_{i}^{2}
and the integration over its posterior in (
7
) that calibrates the p-values at every realized value of
S_{i}^{2}
. (Such conditional calibration is even more important in the
limma-trend
setting, see below.)

2.2
Probabilistic interpretation of
limma-trend

Extending Section
2.1
to
limma-trend
requires
specifying the conditional prior of
\sigma_{i}^{2}
given
M_{i}
. We treat
two cases:
M_{i}
as external side-information to model (
1
), where
the extension is direct, and
M_{i}=A_{i}
the average intensity in (
3
), where
two further issues arise.

Limma-trend
with external side-information.

Suppose
that
M_{i}
is external unit-specific side-information, e.g.,
M_{i}
could be the peptide count covariate in Fig
1
(c), in that the distributional result in (
4
) continues to hold conditional on
M_{i}
:

(Z_{i},S^{2}_{i})\mid\beta_{i},\sigma_{i}^{2},M_{i}\,\sim\mathrm{N}(\theta_{i},\,\nu^{2}\sigma_{i}^{2})\otimes\frac{\sigma_{i}^{2}}{K-p}\chi^{2}_{K-p}.

(8)

As before, for each unit there are two unknown parameters (
\theta_{i}
and
\sigma_{i}^{2}
) and following the partially Bayes principle, the nuisance parameter
\sigma_{i}^{2}
is assumed to follow a prior
G
that is now a function of
M_{i}
, i.e.,
\sigma_{i}^{2}\mid M_{i}\sim G(\cdot\mid M_{i})
. This conditional prior is specified as follows in
limma-trend
; see
also
38
:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sigma_{i}^{2}}\mid M_{i}\;\sim\;\frac{\chi^{2}_{\kappa_{0}}}{\kappa_{0}s_{0}^{2}\xi^{2}_{0}(M_{i})},

(9)

where
\xi_{0}(\cdot)
is a smooth trend and
\kappa_{0},s_{0}^{2}>0
are analogous to (
5
). In Section
3.1
we will work primarily with the nonparametric generalization:

\sigma_{i}^{2}\mid M_{i}\;\sim\;\xi_{0}^{2}(M_{i})G,\quad\mbox{where $G$ is specified nonparametrically.}

(10)

Taking logarithms and writing
m(\cdot)=\log(\xi^{2}_{0}(\cdot))
, this model can be rewritten as
\log(\sigma_{i}^{2})=m(M_{i})+\eta_{i}
, where
\eta_{i}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}M_{i}
and
\exp(\eta_{i})\sim G
. In particular, we additively decompose
\log(\sigma_{i}^{2})
into a part that is fully explained by
M_{i}
and
unexplained remaining independent heterogeneity. In this sense, this model is similar to related empirical Bayes models in the Gaussian sequence model that incorporate external side-information
(
17
;
23
)
.

Given (
8
) and (
9
) (or its nonparametric extension in (
10
)), we interpret
limma-trend
as computing partially Bayes p-values in analogy to (
7
), now conditioning on both
S_{i}^{2}
and
M_{i}
,

P_{i}^{\texttt{ltrd}}:=2\int\Phi(-|Z_{i}|/\{\nu\sigma_{i}\})\Pi(\mathrm{d}\sigma_{i}^{2}\mid S_{i}^{2},M_{i}).

(11)

In practice, we estimate
\xi_{0}(\cdot)
by fitting a trend function
\widehat{\xi}(\cdot)
using
\log(S_{i}^{2})
and
M_{i}
(as in Step 1 of Algorithm
1
) and plug in the estimated trend to estimate
\widehat{G}
in (
10
) (as in Step 2 of Algorithm
1
). Then,
\widehat{\xi}
and
\widehat{G}
yield an estimated posterior
\widehat{\Pi}(\,\cdot\mid S_{i}^{2},M_{i})
and estimated p-values
\widehat{P}_{i}^{\texttt{ltrd}}
, for
i=1,\ldots,n
(Step 3 of Algorithm
1
). In Supplement
I
we show that this general formulation encompasses parametric
limma-trend
as a special case (see Algorithm
2
for our proposed nonparametric version of Algorithm
1
).

As in the untrended case, the oracle p-values
in (
11
) are conditionally uniform under the
null, now given both
S_{i}^{2}
and
M_{i}
:

P_{i}^{\texttt{ltrd}}\mid(\theta_{i}=0,S_{i}^{2},M_{i})\sim\mathrm{Unif}[0,1]
; see Lemma
5
below.
The additional conditioning on
M_{i}
ensures calibration within each stratum of units sharing
M_{i}
, a quantity that the analyst observes directly. For instance, in proteomics,
57

observe that without adjusting for a mean-variance trend, analyses
produce excess false positives among proteins quantified with few
peptides (small
M_{i}
) and excess false negatives among those with many (large
M_{i}
).
Conditioning on
M_{i}
prevents this; we revisit this issue in our
proteomics application in Section
6.3
.

Reg-NPMLE
with average intensity.

As we mentioned earlier, the most common choice of
M_{i}
for
limma-trend
is the average intensity
A_{i}
defined in (
3
) whose distribution under (
1
) satisfies

A_{i}\mid\beta_{i},\sigma_{i}^{2}\,\sim\,\mathrm{N}\left(\mu_{i},\,\frac{1}{K}\sigma_{i}^{2}\right),\;\text{ where }\;\mu_{i}:=c_{A}^{\top}\beta_{i},\;\;c_{A}:=\frac{1}{K}\sum_{j=1}^{K}x_{j}.

(12)

When using
A_{i}
,
limma-trend
proceeds exactly as described above for the case of external side-information. This poses a conceptual challenge for two reasons:

(I)

A_{i}
is computed on the same outcomes
Y_{ij}
in (
1
) as
Z_{i}
and
S_{i}^{2}
, so (
8
) need not hold for
M_{i}=A_{i}
.

(II)

The distributional specifications are circular: the prior of
\sigma_{i}^{2}
is specified as a function of
M_{i}=A_{i}
in (
9
) (and (
10
)), while the likelihood of
A_{i}
in (
12
) depends on
\sigma_{i}^{2}
.

Section
2.3
resolves (I) via an orthogonality
condition on the design (Assumption
1
) that restores independence of
A_{i}
from
(Z_{i},S_{i}^{2})
;
Section
2.4
resolves (II) by treating

(\mu_{i},\sigma_{i}^{2})
jointly as nuisance parameters with a bivariate
prior, which also motivates the
Joint-NPMLE
procedure of
Section
4
.

2.3
Independence, orthogonal contrasts, and average intensity

We first explain that (I) does not pose a concern as long
as the analyst verifies a simple condition on the design and contrast in (
1
). It will be convenient to state our results under slightly more generality. Fix
c_{\widetilde{A}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and let

\widetilde{A}_{i}:=c_{\widetilde{A}}^{\top}\widehat{\beta}_{i}
.
Note that with the choice
c_{\widetilde{A}}=c_{A}
defined in (
12
), we recover the average intensity defined in (
3
), i.e.,
\widetilde{A}_{i}=A_{i}
.

Assumption 1

(Design)
.

The ones vector lies in the column space of
X
, i.e.,
\mathbf{1}\in\mathcal{C}(X)
, and the primary contrast
c_{\theta}
satisfies

c_{\theta}^{\top}(X^{\top}X)^{-1}c_{\widetilde{A}}=0
.

It is straightforward to empirically check this assumption before running a
limma-trend
analysis.
We have the following result:

Proposition 2
.

Suppose that Assumption
1
holds. Then,
(Z_{i},S_{i}^{2},\widetilde{A}_{i})
are mutually independent conditional on
\beta_{i},\sigma_{i}^{2}
, and so in particular, (
8
) holds with
M_{i}=\widetilde{A}_{i}
.

The simplest, yet prevalent, situation that satisfies this assumption is the two-sample comparison.

Example 3

(Two-sample comparison)
.

We seek to test equality of means for each unit between
K_{1}
treatment and
K_{2}
control subjects, with
K=K_{1}+K_{2}
. We encode
x_{j}=(1,0)^{\top}
for
j=1,\ldots,K_{1}
and
x_{j}=(0,1)^{\top}
for
j=K_{1}+1,\ldots,K
. Our contrast of interest is
c_{\theta}=(1,-1)^{\top}
so that

\widehat{\theta}_{i}=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{Y}_{i,\mathrm{treated}}-\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{Y}_{i,\mathrm{control}}
, where
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{Y}_{i,\mathrm{treated}}
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{Y}_{i,\mathrm{control}}
denote the treated/control group means for the
i
-th unit. The average intensity in (
3
) can be written as
A_{i}=(K_{1}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{Y}_{i,\mathrm{treated}}+K_{2}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{Y}_{i,\mathrm{control}})/K
.
In this case,
c_{\theta}
and
c_{A}
satisfy Assumption
1
.

The two-sample comparison generalizes to the following commonly used linear model in which the analyst also adjusts for other variables, e.g., confounders.

Example 4

(Treatment effect)
.

Consider the linear model
Y_{ij}=\alpha_{i}+\tau_{i}w_{j}+\widetilde{x}_{j}^{\top}\gamma_{i}+\varepsilon_{ij},
where
w_{j}\in\{0,1\}
is a treatment indicator,
\widetilde{x}_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
is a vector of control variables, and an intercept is included. Let
X
denote the corresponding design matrix and let
c
denote the contrast vector selecting the coefficient
\tau_{i}
.
Then
c^{\top}(X^{\top}X)^{-1}c_{A}=0
.
Consequently, our analysis of
limma-trend
extends to testing for the significance of treatment effects through the hypotheses
H_{i}:\tau_{i}=0
.

Most applications of
limma-trend
we have encountered indeed satisfy Assumption
1
. Nevertheless, as far as we are aware, Assumption
1
has not been spelled out explicitly before.
This is of import.
As one example, in MAnorm2
(
51
)
, a popular method for two-sample ChIP-Seq comparisons, the average intensity
A_{i}
is replaced by
\widetilde{A}_{i}^{\mathrm{MA2}}:=(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{Y}_{i,\mathrm{treated}}+\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{Y}_{i,\mathrm{control}})/2
with the motivation of “alleviating the influence of unbalanced group sizes.” (Note that
\widetilde{A}_{i}^{\mathrm{MA2}}=A_{i}
when
K_{A}=K_{B}
but they are unequal otherwise.) This choice of
\widetilde{A}_{i}
does not satisfy Assumption
1
, and we show in simulations (Section
5
) that MAnorm2 can lead to substantial inflation of the FDR.

2.4
Partially Bayes with average intensity: bivariate nuisances

We now clarify how to reconcile (
9
) (or its nonparametric generalization in (
10
)) with
M_{i}=A_{i}
and (
12
). We posit Assumption
1
holds for
c_{A}:=\frac{1}{K}\sum_{j=1}^{K}x_{j}
. Then by Proposition
2
,
Z_{i},S_{i}^{2},A_{i}
, are mutually independent and their distribution only depends on
\theta_{i},\mu_{i},\sigma_{i}^{2}
:

\displaystyle(Z_{i},S_{i}^{2},A_{i})\mid(\beta_{i},\sigma_{i}^{2})\,\sim\mathrm{N}(\theta_{i},\,\nu^{2}\sigma_{i}^{2})\otimes\frac{\sigma_{i}^{2}}{K-p}\chi^{2}_{K-p}\otimes\mathrm{N}\left(\mu_{i},\,\frac{1}{K}\sigma_{i}^{2}\right).

(13)

We will base inference for the primary parameter
\theta_{i}
only on
Z_{i},S_{i}^{2},A_{i}
. Thus, we will treat
\mu_{i}
and
\sigma_{i}^{2}
as the nuisance parameters. Following the partially Bayes principle, we posit that
(\mu_{i},\sigma_{i}^{2})
are drawn from an unknown bivariate prior, while
\theta_{i}
is treated as fixed,

(\mu_{i},\sigma_{i}^{2})\sim H,\text{ and }\theta_{i}\,\text{ is deterministic,}\;\qquad i=1,\ldots,n.

(14)

Now, under (
13
) and (
14
), the posterior distribution of
\sigma_{i}^{2}
given
A_{i}
is well-defined and (implicitly) marginalizes over
\mu_{i}
. We say that
H
is
compatible
with
limma-trend
if the aforementioned posterior takes the form in (
10
) with
M_{i}=A_{i}
. As an example, in Proposition
S7
in the supplement, we show that the conjugate Gaussian/Inv
\chi^{2}
prior
H
is compatible.

The above interpretation has two important consequences in this paper. First, in Section
3.2

we show that our proposed
Reg-NPMLE
procedure asymptotically controls the FDR
even
if
H
in (
14
) is not compatible; this is a strong form of robustness. However, without compatibility, the procedure loses its full partially Bayes interpretation in that case. To address this, in Section
4
we develop an alternative procedure that directly targets the prior in (
14
), thereby retaining a fully partially Bayes interpretation even when
H
is not compatible.

3
Reg-NPMLE
:
limma-trend
based on the NPMLE

Throughout this section, we make the following assumption:

\displaystyle(Z_{i},S^{2}_{i})\mid\theta_{i},\sigma_{i}^{2},M_{i}\sim\mathrm{N}\left(\theta_{i},\nu^{2}\sigma_{i}^{2}\right)\otimes\frac{\sigma_{i}^{2}}{K-p}\chi^{2}_{K-p}.

(15)

Our formulation of (
15
) captures two models. First,
M_{i}
can be external side-information (cf. Section
2.1
), so that the above model corresponds to (
8
), noting that the conditional distributions depend on
\beta_{i}
only through
\theta_{i}
. Second, we allow for
M_{i}=A_{i}
, the average intensity, positing that Assumption
1
holds, and moreover, that
(\mu_{i},\sigma_{i}^{2})\sim H
as in (
14
). In the latter case, (
15
) must be interpreted as marginalizing over
\mu_{i}
(as described in Section
2.4
).

3.1
Well-specified
Reg-NPMLE

We now consider our nonparametric version of
limma-trend
. In addition to (
15
),
we posit (
10
) which we restate here: we posit that
there exists a function
\xi_{0}(\cdot)
such that:

\tau_{i}^{2}\mid M_{i}\,\sim\,G,\;\text{ where }\;\tau_{i}^{2}:=\sigma_{i}^{2}/\xi_{0}^{2}(M_{i}),\;\text{ and }\theta_{i}\text{ are deterministic}.

(16)

Both
\xi_{0}^{2}
and
G
are unknown. This model encompasses external side information, and the average intensity, in which case the bivariate prior
H
in (
14
) must be compatible (as defined in Section
2.4
).

Using the notation of (
16
), we can equivalently express (
9
) as
(\tau_{i}^{2})^{-1}\mid M_{i}\sim\chi^{2}_{\kappa_{0}}/(\kappa_{0}\,s_{0}^{2})
. Our goal is to replace this parametric assumption by the assumption that
G\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{tr}}
, where
\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{tr}}
is a nonparametric class of distributions. To this end, we start by rewriting the oracle partially Bayes p-values
P_{i}^{\texttt{ltrd}}
as an explicit function of
G
and
\xi_{0}
in (
15
), namely,
2
2

2

We specialize
P_{i}^{\texttt{ltrd}}
in three different settings, each time introducing new notation (e.g.,
P^{\mathsf{tr}}_{i}
below) to indicate that the p-values pertain to the setting at hand.

\displaystyle P^{\mathsf{tr}}_{i}:=\frac{\int_{0}^{\infty}2\,\Phi\!\left(-\frac{|Z_{i}|}{\nu\,\xi_{0}(M_{i})\tau_{i}}\right)\,p_{\chi^{2}}(S^{2}_{i}\mid K-p,\xi^{2}_{0}(M_{i})\tau^{2}_{i})\,G(\mathrm{d}\tau^{2}_{i})}{\int_{0}^{\infty}p_{\chi^{2}}(S^{2}_{i}\mid K-p,\xi^{2}_{0}(M_{i})\tau^{2}_{i})\,G(\mathrm{d}\tau^{2}_{i})},\qquad\mbox{for $i\in[n]$,}

(17)

where
p_{\chi^{2}}(\,\cdot\,\mid K-p,\tau^{2})
is the density of
\tau^{2}\chi^{2}_{K-p}/(K-p)
random variable.

The following lemma shows that
P^{\mathsf{tr}}_{i}
are uniform conditional on
S_{i}^{2},M_{i}
, a desirable property (see Section
2.1
) implied by the partially Bayes specification.

Lemma 5
.

Under the data generating model (
15
) and (
16
),
for all
i\in[n]
such that
\theta_{i}=0
, we have

\mathbb{P}\left[P^{\mathsf{tr}}_{i}\leq t\mid S^{2}_{i},M_{i}\right]=t,\;\mbox{for all $t\in[0,1]$, almost surely.}

Given the form of the oracle partially Bayes p-values in (
17
), it remains to explain how to estimate
\xi_{0}
and
G
.
We will state our results below in terms of a general estimator
\widehat{\xi}(\cdot)
that is consistent for
\xi_{0}(\cdot)
, that is, we do not prescribe a specific estimation strategy for this step. However, in practice, we use the approach implemented in the
limma
package
(
40
)
using natural splines (see, Step 1 of Algorithm
1
and Supplement
B.1
).
Next, we focus on estimating
G
from
\{(S^{2}_{i},M_{i}):i\in[n]\}
. The marginal density of
V^{2}_{i}:=S^{2}_{i}/\xi^{2}_{0}(M_{i})
conditioned on
M_{i}
is given by

\displaystyle f_{G,K-p}(v^{2})=\int_{0}^{\infty}p_{\chi^{2}}(v^{2}\mid K-p,\tau^{2})\,G(\mathrm{d}\tau^{2}).

(18)

Thus, if
\xi_{0}
were known, we could compute the NPMLE that optimize the joint marginal log-likelihood of
V^{2}_{1},\ldots,V^{2}_{n}
over all
G\in\mathcal{G}_{\operatorname{tr}}
. Instead, we compute the same NPMLE with
V_{i}^{2}
replaced by plug-in estimates
\widehat{V}_{i}^{2}
that use
\widehat{\xi}
in lieu of the true
\xi_{0}
:

\displaystyle\widehat{G}_{\mathsf{tr}}\in\argmax_{G\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{tr}}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log f_{G,K-p}\!\left(\widehat{V}^{2}_{i}\right),\;\;\text{ where }\;\widehat{V}^{2}_{i}:=S^{2}_{i}/\widehat{\xi}^{2}(M_{i}).

(19)

We emphasize that the estimator
\widehat{G}_{\mathsf{tr}}
is not an NPMLE per se, but rather an approximation to an oracle NPMLE that knows
V_{i}^{2}
(and our theory will account for this discrepancy). We observe (
19
) is a convex optimization that can be efficiently solved using the proposal of
27
. More specifically, we solve (
19
) through approximating
G^{\prime}
using a discrete prior and reducing (
19
) to a conic programming problem, which
is solved using MOSEK
(
37
)
.
Further details are provided in Supplement
B.2
.
We note that our theory does not account for discretization and for the data-driven choice of the support of the prior class.
Previous works,
11
and
47
, have conducted analyses of the NPMLE while considering the effects of discretization.

Using the estimated trends
\widehat{\xi}
and prior
\widehat{G}_{\mathsf{tr}}
, one can estimate
P^{\mathsf{tr}}_{i}
using
\widehat{P}^{\mathsf{tr}}_{i}
, where for all
i\in[n]
, the estimators are computed using (
17
) by replacing
\xi_{0}
and
G
with
\widehat{\xi}
and
\widehat{G}_{\mathsf{tr}}
, respectively. Our full proposal is summarized in Algorithm
2
:

Algorithm 2

limma-trend
with nonparametric prior (
Reg-NPMLE
)

1:

Fit the trend of
\log(S_{i}^{2})
versus
M_{i}
, call this trend
\widehat{m}(\cdot)
and its exponential
\widehat{\xi}^{2}(\cdot)=\exp(\widehat{m}(\cdot))
.

2:

Estimate
G
as
\widehat{G}_{\mathsf{tr}}
using the approximate NPMLE with plugged-in
\widehat{\xi}
as in (
19
).

3:

Compute the p-values
\widehat{P}^{\mathsf{tr}}_{i}
using (
17
) by replacing
\xi_{0}
with
\widehat{\xi}
and
G
with
\widehat{G}_{\mathsf{tr}}
.

We now develop theory for
Reg-NPMLE
. We assume the following on the data-generating process.

Assumption 6
.

The tuples
(\tau_{i}^{2},M_{i},Z_{i},S_{i}^{2})
are generated according to (
15
) and (
16
) for
K-p\geq 2
and are jointly independendent across
i\in[n]
.
We have that

\mathbb{P}\!\left(\max_{i\in[n]}|M_{i}|>\mathrm{W}\sqrt{\log n}\right)\leq n^{-3}
for some
W>0
. The trend-adjusted variance prior satisfies
G\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{tr}}
, where
\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{tr}}:=\left\{G^{\prime}:G^{\prime}\left([\underline{L}_{\mathsf{tr}},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{U}_{\mathsf{tr}}]\right)=1\right\}
for absolute constants
\underline{L}_{\mathsf{tr}},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{U}_{\mathsf{tr}}>0
and the true trend function
\xi_{0}
satisfies
\xi_{0}(x)\in[\underline{M}_{\mathsf{tr}},\overline{M}_{\mathsf{tr}}]
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
, where
\underline{M}_{\mathsf{tr}},\overline{M}_{\mathsf{tr}}>0
are absolute constants.

The choice of nonparametric class
\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{tr}}
makes no smoothness assumptions whatsoever, but it does impose lower and upper bounds (see the discussion following Assumption 8 in
22
).
We next make a high-level assumption on the estimation of the trend.

Assumption 7

(Trend estimation)
.

Let
\mathrm{W}_{n}:=\mathrm{W}\sqrt{\log n}
for
\mathrm{W}
defined in Assumption
6
. For any two functions
f,g
, define the semi-norm
\|f-g\|_{\mathrm{W}_{n}}:=\sup_{x\in[-\mathrm{W}_{n},\mathrm{W}_{n}]}|f(x)-g(x)|
. We assume that the estimated trend
\widehat{\xi}(\cdot)
satisfies

\|\widehat{\xi}-\xi_{0}\|_{\mathrm{W}_{n}}\leq\Delta_{n}
,
where
\Delta_{n}=o(1)
is a deterministic sequence. Moreover, we assume that
\widehat{\xi}\in\mathcal{X}
almost surely, where
\mathcal{X}
is a class of functions that contains
\xi_{0}
and is separable under the supremum norm on
\mathbb{R}
. Furthermore, all
\xi\in\mathcal{X}
satisfy
\xi(\cdot)\in[\underline{M}_{\mathsf{tr}},\overline{M}_{\mathsf{tr}}]
and
\log N(\varepsilon,\mathcal{X},\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{W}_{n}})\lesssim(1/\varepsilon)^{h_{1}}\cdot|\log\varepsilon|^{h_{2}}
for some constants
h_{1},h_{2}\in[0,1]
,
where
N(\varepsilon,\mathcal{X},\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{W}_{n}})
is the
\varepsilon
-covering number of the class
\mathcal{X}
in
\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{W}_{n}}
.

Our main result quantifies the rate at which we approximate the oracle partially Bayes p-values and accounts for the combined impact of prior approximation and trend estimation error.

Theorem 8
.

Suppose Assumptions
6
and
7
hold. Then, for any
\zeta\in\left(\frac{1}{2},1\right)
, there exists a constant
\mathrm{C}_{1}>0
, depending on
\underline{M}_{\mathsf{tr}},\overline{M}_{\mathsf{tr}},\underline{L}_{\mathsf{tr}},\overline{U}_{\mathsf{tr}},K,p,\nu
, and
\zeta
, such that,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left|P^{\mathsf{tr}}_{i}\wedge\zeta-\widehat{P}_{i}^{\mathsf{tr}}\wedge\zeta\right|\right]\;\leq\;\mathrm{C}_{1}\cdot\mathfrak{L}_{n}(\Delta_{n})\,\text{ for all }\,n\geq 1,

(20)

where

\mathfrak{L}_{n}(\Delta_{n}):=\max\left\{\Delta_{n}\log^{5/2}n,\,\frac{\log^{5/2}n}{n^{1/4}}\Delta^{1/2}_{n},\,\frac{\log^{2}n}{n^{1/4}}|\log\Delta_{n}|^{h_{2}/4}\Delta^{\frac{1}{2}(1-h_{1}/2)}_{n},\,\frac{\log^{5/2}n}{n^{1/2}}\right\}.

If
\mathcal{X}
contains parametric functions, then
h_{1}=0
and
h_{2}=1
. In that case, the optimal function estimation rate is
\Delta_{n}=n^{-1/2}
and
\mathfrak{L}_{n}(\Delta_{n})
is also equal to
n^{-1/2}
up to log factors. Similarly, if
\mathcal{X}
is
\varrho
-Hölder smooth on
[-\mathrm{W}_{n},\mathrm{W}_{n}]
for
\varrho\geq 1
, then we can take
h_{1}=1/\varrho
and
h_{2}\geq 0
and our result yields
\mathfrak{L}_{n}(\Delta_{n})
of order
n^{-\varrho/(2\varrho+1)}
(up to log factors), which is also the minimax rate for estimating
\xi_{0}
.
Thus, the statistical cost of p-value estimation matches (and is dominated by) that of the trend function estimation up to logarithmic factors for parametric and Hölder-smooth trends.

Remark 9

(Proof strategy: marginal density convergence)
.

The proof has two primary ingredients. First, we control the squared Hellinger distance between the true and fitted marginal densities (Lemma
S9
),

\mathcal{H}^{2}\left(f_{G,K-p},f_{\widehat{G}_{\mathsf{tr}},K-p}\right):=\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\sqrt{f_{G,K-p}(x)}-\sqrt{f_{\widehat{G}_{\mathsf{tr}},K-p}(x)}\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}x,

accounting for the fact that
\widehat{G}_{\mathsf{tr}}
is an approximate NPMLE under a misspecified likelihood in which the true trend
\xi_{0}
is replaced by an estimate
\widehat{\xi}
; we handle this by extending techniques from
7
. Second, an Eddington–Tweedie-type identity (see (
S127
) of Supplement
J.3
, analogous to Proposition 11 in
22
) expresses the p-values
P^{\mathsf{tr}}_{i}
as functionals of the marginal density, so Hellinger consistency of the marginal transfers directly into rates for the plug-in p-values.

A practical consequence is that our guarantees go through the marginal density, not the latent prior
G
. We therefore recommend visual diagnostics that compare the fitted marginal to the observed data, rather than diagnostics that target
G
itself; see Section
6
.

The following results are established under the same assumptions as Theorem
8
. First, we show that the conditional uniformity of Lemma
5
holds approximately for the estimated p-values.

Proposition 10
.

Suppose Assumptions
6
and
7
hold.
Then, there exists a constant
C_{\mathsf{tr}}>0
depending on
\nu,K,p,\underline{M}_{\mathsf{tr}},\overline{M}_{\mathsf{tr}},\underline{L}_{\mathsf{tr}},\overline{U}_{\mathsf{tr}},\zeta
, such that

\displaystyle\max_{i\in[n]\,:\,\theta_{i}=0}\left\{\mathbb{E}_{G}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,\zeta]}\left|\mathbb{P}_{G}\!\left(\widehat{P}_{i}^{\mathsf{tr}}\leq t\mid\{(S_{j}^{2},M_{j})\}_{j=1}^{n}\right)-t\right|\right]\right\}\leq C_{\mathsf{tr}}\,\mathfrak{L}_{n}(\Delta_{n}).

(21)

In practice,
limma
-type p-values are used alongside the BH procedure
(
2
)
to control the false discovery rate (FDR). For this reason, below we show asymptotic FDR control using BH on
\widehat{P}_{1}^{\mathsf{tr}},\ldots,\widehat{P}_{n}^{\mathsf{tr}}
.

Let us briefly review the BH procedure applied at level
\alpha\in(0,1)
to a generic collection of
p-values
P^{\mathrm{gen}}_{1},\ldots,P^{\mathrm{gen}}_{n}
. One first sorts the p-values as
P^{\mathrm{gen}}_{(1)}\leq P^{\mathrm{gen}}_{(2)}\leq\cdots\leq P^{\mathrm{gen}}_{(n)}
and determines the largest
j
(called,
\widehat{j}
) such that
P^{\mathrm{gen}}_{(j)}\leq(j/n)\alpha
. If no such
j
exists, then
\widehat{j}=0
. Finally, the hypotheses corresponding to
P^{\mathrm{gen}}_{(1)},\cdots,P^{\mathrm{gen}}_{\left(\widehat{j}\right)}
are rejected. If the p-values are super uniform and independent, then this procedure controls the FDR at
\alpha

(
2
)
.

To provide asymptotic results on the BH procedure, we introduce the following definition.

Definition 11
.

A sequence
\{P^{\mathrm{gen}}_{i}:i\in[n]\}
is
critically dense
at
\alpha\in(0,1)
, if there exists
0<t_{0}(\alpha)<t_{1}(\alpha)<\alpha
such that

\smash{\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\inf_{t\in[t_{0},t_{1}]}\left\{(nt)^{-1}\penalty\ \sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{P}\!\left[P^{\mathrm{gen}}_{i}\leq t\right]\right\}\penalty\ >\penalty\ \alpha^{-1}.}

This criticality assumption is closely related to the standard asymptotic setup of
48
. Under weak dependence on the
P^{\mathrm{gen}}_{i}
, it implies that BH asymptotically rejects all p-values below a data-driven threshold
\widehat{t}\geq t_{0}
; also see
22
.

In our setting, we apply BH at level
\alpha
to
\widehat{P}_{1}^{\mathsf{tr}},\ldots,\widehat{P}_{n}^{\mathsf{tr}}
, which are only approximate p-values. We denote the number of false discoveries by
V_{n}^{\mathsf{tr}}
, by
the total number of discoveries
R_{n}^{\mathsf{tr}}
, and the FDR by
\operatorname{FDR}^{\mathsf{tr}}_{n}:=\mathbb{E}\left[V^{\mathsf{tr}}_{n}/(R^{\mathsf{tr}}_{n}\vee 1)\right].
Our main FDR control results is the following.

Theorem 12
.

Fix
\alpha\in(0,1)
. Suppose Assumptions
6
and
7
hold and the oracle partially Bayes p-value sequence
\{P^{\mathsf{tr}}_{i}\}
is critically dense at
\alpha
in the sense of Definition
11
. Let
n_{0}:=\#\{i\in[n]:\theta_{i}=0\}
. Then, for any sequence
\vartheta_{n}>0
with

\smash{\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathfrak{L}_{n}(\Delta_{n})\vartheta_{n}=0,}

we have

\lim_{n\to\infty}\left\{\vartheta_{n}\left(\operatorname{FDR}_{n}^{\mathsf{tr}}-\frac{n_{0}}{n}\alpha\right)_{+}\right\}=0.

We emphasize that we impose the criticality condition on the oracle p-values
P^{\mathsf{tr}}_{i}
; the procedure we study uses the estimated p-values
\widehat{P}^{\mathsf{tr}}_{i}
.

3.2
Misspecified
Reg-NPMLE

In this section, we study the robustness of Algorithm
2
when the model (
16
) is misspecified or when Assumption
7
fails and
\widehat{\xi}
is not even consistent for
\xi_{0}
. The reason is two-fold: first, (
16
) can be violated in some settings (e.g., in our proteomics dataset from Section
6.3
), yet
limma-trend
is still used in practice, raising the question of whether it retains any inferential guarantees. Second, it is common to impose effectively parametric assumptions when estimating trends (e.g., the trend estimation in Supplement
B.1
is effectively parametric) and such parametric trends are likely to be misspecified.
In particular, we continue to assume (
15
) and replace (
16
) by the following:

M_{i}\sim\mathbb{P}^{M},\;\;\sigma_{i}^{2}\mid M_{i}\sim G_{M_{i}}(\cdot\mid M_{i}),\text{ and }\theta_{i}\text{ is deterministic}.

(22)

Thus, we now allow for the conditional distribution of
\sigma_{i}^{2}
to depend in an arbitrarily complicated way on
M_{i}
. (We explicitly define the distribution
\mathbb{P}^{M}
of
M_{i}
here because it will be needed below.)

Meanwhile, we replace Assumption
7
by the following.

Assumption 13

(Misspecified trend estimation)
.

There exists a deterministic function
\xi_{\mathrm{mis}}
such that

\|\widehat{\xi}-\xi_{\mathrm{mis}}\|_{\infty}\leq\Delta_{n}
where
\Delta_{n}=o(1)
. Moreover, the estimated trend satisfies the regularity conditions of Assumption
7
(that is, formally replacing
\xi_{0}
by
\xi_{\mathrm{mis}}
in the statement therein).

Even when (
16
) holds, Assumption
13
allows for
\xi_{\mathrm{mis}}\neq\xi_{0}
, which implies that
\widehat{\xi}
is an inconsistent estimator of
\xi_{0}
. More generally,
\xi_{\mathrm{mis}}
is not tied to the data-generating process (beyond the fact that
\widehat{\xi}
converges to
\xi_{\mathrm{mis}}
). Given
\xi_{\mathrm{mis}}
, we also define:

\tau_{i,\mathrm{mis}}^{2}:=\sigma_{i}^{2}/\xi_{\mathrm{mis}}^{2}(M_{i}),\;\;V_{i,\mathrm{mis}}^{2}:=S_{i}^{2}/\xi_{\mathrm{mis}}^{2}(M_{i}).

(23)

With these definition in place, we can replace Assumption
6
by the following.

Assumption 14
.

The tuples
(\tau_{i,\mathrm{mis}}^{2},M_{i},Z_{i},V_{i,\mathrm{mis}}^{2})
are generated according to (
15
), (
22
) and (
23
) for
K-p\geq 2
and are jointly independendent across
i\in[n]
.
Furthermore, the random variables
M_{1},\ldots,M_{n}
satisfy

\mathbb{P}\!\left(\max_{i\in[n]}|M_{i}|>\mathrm{W}\sqrt{\log n}\right)\leq n^{-3}
for some
W>0
.
Moreover, we optimize
G
in (
19
) over

\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{tr}}=[\underline{L}_{\mathsf{tr}},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{U}_{\mathsf{tr}}]

for absolute constants
\underline{L}_{\mathsf{tr}},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{U}_{\mathsf{tr}}>0
that are such that
\tau_{i,\mathrm{mis}}^{2}\in[\underline{L}_{\mathsf{tr}},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{U}_{\mathsf{tr}}]
almost surely.

Now let us call
P_{i,\mathrm{mis}}^{\mathsf{tr}}
to be the p-values in (
17
) with
(G,\xi_{0})
replaced by
(G_{\mathrm{mis}},\xi_{\mathrm{mis}})
, where
G_{\mathrm{mis}}
is the unconditional distribution of
\tau_{i,\mathrm{mis}}^{2}
(implied by the joint distribution of
(M_{i},\sigma_{i}^{2})
in (
22
)).
Let
\operatorname{FDR}^{\mathrm{mis}}_{n}
be the FDR of BH at level
\alpha
applied to
\widehat{P}_{i}^{\mathsf{tr}}
in the present misspecified setting.
Then, similarly to the well-specified case (Theorem
12
), FDR is asymptotically controlled.

Theorem 15
.

Fix
\alpha\in(0,1)
. Consider Assumptions
13
and
14
for
G_{\mathrm{mis}},\xi_{\mathrm{mis}}
and
\widehat{\xi}
. Assume that
n_{0}:=\#\{i\in[n]:\theta_{i}=0\}
satisfies
n_{0}/n\rightarrow\pi_{0}\in(0,1)
as
n\rightarrow\infty
.
If the sequence
\{P^{\mathsf{tr}}_{i,\mathrm{mis}}:i\in[n]\}
is critically dense at
\alpha
,
then

\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\operatorname{FDR}^{\mathrm{mis}}_{n}\leq\pi_{0}\,\alpha.

The above theorem is the main robustness result of this section. The intuition behind it is that the (oracle) misspecified p-values
P_{i,\mathrm{mis}}^{\mathsf{tr}}
retain a partially Bayes interpretation analogous as in Lemma
5
.

Lemma 16
.

Suppose that (
15
) and (
22
) hold. Fix
\xi_{\mathrm{mis}}
and let
V_{i,\mathrm{mis}}
be defined as in (
23
). Then, for all
i\in[n]
such that
\theta_{i}=0
, we have that
\mathbb{P}[P^{\mathsf{tr}}_{i,\mathrm{mis}}\leq t\mid V^{2}_{i,\mathrm{mis}}]=t
for all
t\in[0,1]
almost surely.

In particular, whatever the misspecified trend
\xi_{\mathrm{mis}}
is,
3
3

3

The power of the p-values, however, depends critically on the misspecified trend
\xi_{\mathrm{mis}}
.

G_{\mathrm{mis}}
“buffers” the error so that uniformity holds conditional on oracle trend-adjusted variances
V_{i,\mathrm{mis}}
. While stronger than unconditional uniformity and sufficient for asymptotic FDR control, Lemma
16
is not as strong as Lemma
5
and does not allow us to argue, e.g., in the application of Section
6.3
that p-values are calibrated for proteins with small peptide count.
Finally, we note that Proposition
S13
in the supplement, establishes an asymptotic version of Lemma
16
for the estimated p-values
\widehat{P}^{\mathsf{tr}}_{i}
.

4
Joint-NPMLE
:
limma-trend
with bivariate nuisance priors

Algorithm
2
(
Reg-NPMLE
) in its oracle form ensures the partially Bayes property of Lemma
5
only when (
16
) is well-specified. However, that model takes a shortcut: it captures the conditional distribution of
\sigma_{i}^{2}\mid M_{i}
through only a trend and a trend-adjusted variance prior, rather than modeling the full conditional prior. To go beyond this, we must learn that full conditional prior directly. In Supplement
A
and Algorithm
S1
therein, we show how this can be accomplished when
M_{i}
is discrete (as in our proteomics application).

In this section, we provide a construction for the average intensity,
M_{i}=A_{i}
.
Throughout, we assume data are generated according to (
13
) and (
14
) (which implicity also impose Assumption
1
). The main crux is that instead of directly learning the conditional distribution given
M_{i}
,
it suffices to learn the bivariate nuisance prior
H
.
We rewrite the oracle p-values
P_{i}^{\texttt{ltrd}}
in (
11
) in terms of
H
,

P^{\text{jt}}_{i}:=\frac{\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}2\,\Phi\!\left(-\frac{|Z_{i}|}{\nu\,\sigma}\right)\,p_{K-p}(S^{2}_{i},A_{i}\mid\mu,\sigma^{2})\,H(\mathrm{d}\mu,\mathrm{d}\sigma^{2})}{\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}p_{K-p}(S^{2}_{i},A_{i}\mid\mu,\sigma^{2})\,H(\mathrm{d}\mu,\mathrm{d}\sigma^{2})},\qquad\mbox{for $i\in[n]$,}

(24)

where
p_{\kappa}(s^{2},a\mid\mu,\sigma^{2}):=p_{\chi^{2}}(s^{2}\mid\kappa,\sigma^{2})\,\phi\!\left(\sqrt{K}(a-\mu)/\sigma\right)

and
\phi(\cdot)
is the standard Gaussian density.
The following proposition establishes conditional uniformity for
P^{\text{jt}}_{i}
analogous to that for
P_{i}^{\mathsf{tr}}
in Lemma
5
.

Lemma 17
.

For all
i\in[n]
such that
\theta_{i}=0
, we have
\mathbb{P}_{H}[P^{\mathrm{jt}}_{i}\leq t\mid S^{2}_{i},A_{i}]=t,\quad\mbox{for all $t\in[0,1]$.}

We propose to estimate
H
from the data by NPMLE using the marginal distribution of the summary statistics
\{(S^{2}_{i},A_{i}):i\in[n]\}
over a nonparametric class
\mathcal{G}_{H}
,

\displaystyle\widehat{H}\in\argmax_{H^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}_{H}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log f_{H^{\prime},K-p}(S^{2}_{i},A_{i}).

(25)

Here, for any bivariate mixing measure
H^{\prime}
, we write

\displaystyle f_{H^{\prime},\kappa}(s^{2},a):=\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}p_{\kappa}(s^{2},a\mid\mu,\sigma^{2})\,H^{\prime}(\mathrm{d}\mu,\mathrm{d}\sigma^{2}),\quad\mbox{for $\kappa\in\{K-p,K-p+1\}$}.

(26)

We solve (
25
) by approximating
H
through a finite sieve, which yields a finite-dimensional convex
program, and applying the interior point solver MOSEK
(
37
)
; see Supplement
B.3
for details. (Our theory below ignores the approximation error and data-driven choice of the sieve.)

We obtain plug-in estimates
\widehat{P}^{\mathrm{jt}}_{i}
of the oracle p-values
P^{\mathrm{jt}}_{i}
by replacing
H
with
\widehat{H}
in (
24
). Our proposal is summarized in Algorithm
3
.

Algorithm 3

limma-trend
with bivariate nuisances (
Joint-NPMLE
)

1:

Estimate
H
using
\widehat{H}
via the NPMLE of (
25
).

2:

Compute the p-values
\widehat{P}^{\mathrm{jt}}_{i}
using (
24
) while replacing
H
by
\widehat{H}
.

Our theory crucially builds on the following result. The oracle p-values
\{P^{\mathrm{jt}}_{i}\}
satisfy an Eddington-Tweedie type representation in terms of the marginal densities of the summary statistics. (Analogous results are a cornerstone of the empirical Bayes literature, see e.g.,
13
;
10
;
15
;
22
).

Theorem 18
.

Recall
f_{H,K-p}
and
f_{H,K-p+1}
defined in (
26
). There exists
C_{K,p}>0
such that

\displaystyle P^{\mathrm{jt}}_{i}=C_{K,p}\frac{(S^{2}_{i})^{\frac{K-p}{2}-1}}{f_{H,K-p}(S^{2}_{i},A_{i})}\int_{\frac{(K-p)S^{2}_{i}+(Z^{2}_{i}/\nu^{2})}{K-p+1}}^{\infty}\frac{(t^{2})^{-\frac{K-p-1}{2}}f_{H,K-p+1}(t^{2},A_{i})}{\sqrt{(K\!-\!p\!+\!1)t^{2}-(K\!-\!p)S^{2}_{i}}}\,\,\mathrm{d}t^{2}.

(27)

To prove asymptotic validity of the
\{\widehat{P}^{\mathrm{jt}}_{i}\}
to
\{P^{\mathrm{jt}}_{i}\}
s, we make the following assumption.

Assumption 19
.

The tuples
(\mu_{i},\sigma^{2}_{i},Z_{i},S^{2}_{i},A_{i})
are generated according to (
13
) and (
14
) for
K-p\geq 2
and are jointly independendent across
i\in[n]
.
We use the nonparametric class
\mathcal{G}_{H}:=\left\{H^{\prime}:H^{\prime}([-M,M]\times[\underline{L},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{U}])=1\right\}
for absolute constants
M,\underline{L},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{U}>0
. It holds that the data-generating
H
in (
14
) satisfies
H\in\mathcal{G}_{H}
.

Under the above assumption, the Eddington-Tweedie formula from Theorem
18
can be utilized in conjunction with the convergence of
f_{\widehat{H},K-p}
to
f_{H,K-p}
in Hellinger distance and of
f_{\widehat{H},K-p+1}
to
f_{H,K-p+1}
in the
L^{2}
distance (both shown in the supplement), to obtain the following theorem on the convergence of the estimated p-values to the oracle p-values.

Theorem 20
.

Suppose Assumption
19
hold. Then, for all
\zeta\in\left(\frac{1}{2},1\right)
, there exists a constant
C>0
(depending only on
\underline{L},\overline{U},M,K,p,\nu,\zeta
) such that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
,

\max_{1\leq i\leq n}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|P^{\mathrm{jt}}_{i}\wedge\zeta-\widehat{P}^{\mathrm{jt}}_{i}\wedge\zeta\right|\right]\leq C\cdot\frac{\log^{13/4}n}{\sqrt{n}}.

The rate is parametric, even though the procedure implicitly learns the conditional prior of
\sigma_{i}^{2}\mid A_{i}
.
The following results are established under the same assumptions as Theorem
20
. First, we extend the conditional uniformity of Lemma
17
to approximately hold for the estimated p-values.

Proposition 21
.

Suppose Assumption
19
hold. Fix
\zeta\in(1/2,1)
. There exists a constant
C>0
, depending only on
\underline{L},\overline{U},M,K,p,\nu
and
\zeta
, such that for all
n\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\max_{i\in[n]\,:\,\theta_{i}=0}\mathbb{E}_{H}\!\left[\sup_{t\in[0,\zeta]}\left|\mathbb{P}_{H}\!\left(\widehat{P}_{i}^{\mathrm{jt}}\leq t\mid\{(S_{j}^{2},A_{j})\}_{j=1}^{n}\right)-t\right|\right]\leq C\frac{\log^{13/4}n}{\sqrt{n}}.

(28)

Next, let
\operatorname{FDR}_{n}^{\mathrm{jt}}
denote the FDR of the BH procedure at level
\alpha
applied to
\widehat{P}_{1}^{\mathrm{jt}},\ldots,\widehat{P}_{n}^{\mathrm{jt}}
to
to test
H_{i}:\theta_{i}=0
for
i\in[n]
with the nominal level of significance
\alpha\in(0,1)
. The following theorem shows asymptotic FDR control under a criticality condition as in Theorem
12
.

Theorem 22
.

Suppose Assumption
19
hold and the oracle p-values
\{P_{i}^{\mathrm{jt}}\}
are critically dense at
\alpha
as defined in Definition
11
. Let
n_{0}:=\#\{i\in[n]:\theta_{i}=0\}
.
Then, for
\varrho>13/4
,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\left\{n^{1/2}(\log n)^{-\varrho}\left(\operatorname{FDR}_{n}^{\mathrm{jt}}-\frac{n_{0}}{n}\alpha\right)_{+}\right\}=0.

Under slightly stronger assumptions on the number of null hypotheses and the mixing distribution, one can also study the asymptotic power of BH applied to
\widehat{P}_{1}^{\mathrm{jt}},\ldots,\widehat{P}_{n}^{\mathrm{jt}}
. We discuss such power properties in Section
L.7
.

5
Numerical Experiments

We assess our proposals,
Reg-NPMLE
(Algorithm
2
) and
Joint-NPMLE
(Algorithm
3
), against seven alternatives: (a) the
t
-test; (b) untrended
limma

(
46
)
, denoted Untrended-Inv
\chi^{2}
; (c) its NPMLE variant
(
22
)
, Untrended-NPMLE; (d) parametric
Reg-Inv
\chi^{2}
(Algorithm
1
); (e) MAnorm2
(
51
)
; (f) MAP
(
30
)
; and (g) an
Oracle
that computes partially Bayes p-values using the true data-generating distribution. Supplement
B.4
provides more details on the baselines.

All four settings follow the two-sample comparison of Example
3
with
n=10000
independent units and
K=K_{A}+K_{B}
samples. Of the
n
units,
n_{0}=9000
are null (
\theta_{i}=0
); the remaining have
\theta_{i}\sim\mathrm{N}(0,\,16\sigma_{i}^{2})
, where
\sigma_{i}^{2}=\exp\{m(\mu_{i})\}\,\tau_{i}^{2}
and
\tau_{i}^{2}\sim G
. The choices of
(K_{A},K_{B})
, trend
m(\cdot)
, residual prior
G
, and the distribution of
\mu_{i}
are specified for each setting below. In Settings 1–3, the trended methods use
M_{i}=A_{i}
(and
M_{i}=\tilde{A}_{i}^{\mathrm{MA2}}
for MAnorm2); in Setting 4, we provide
\mu_{i}
directly as external side-information
M_{i}=\mu_{i}
. The latter is not feasible in practice (
\mu_{i}
is unknown), but we use it to construct an idealized setting in which the trend perfectly determines the variance. P-values are passed through BH at
\alpha=0.05
, and FDR and power are estimated over
100
Monte Carlo replicates. Results are reported in Table
1
.

:
FDR and power (in %) at nominal level
\alpha=0.05
across the four simulation settings of Section
5
. FDR entries exceeding the nominal level are highlighted in
red
. Settings 1–3 use
M_{i}=A_{i}
(and
\tilde{A}_{i}^{\mathrm{MA2}}
for MAnorm2); Setting 4 treats
M_{i}=\mu_{i}
as external side-information, where MAnorm2 and
Joint-NPMLE
do not apply (“–”).

Across all four settings, our procedures match or beat the feasible competitors in power while remaining the only methods (besides
Oracle
) to control FDR throughout. The trended methods (
Reg-NPMLE
,
Joint-NPMLE
) outperform their untrended counterparts whenever a non-trivial trend is present (Settings 2–4). Additional simulations are reported in Supplement
C
.

•

Setting 1 (parametric prior misspecified, no trend).
We take
K_{A}=K_{B}=3
,
G=0.5\delta_{1}+0.5\delta_{10}
,
m\equiv 0
,
\mu_{i}\sim\mathrm{N}(20,3)
. The parametric prior is misspecified, so Untrended-Inv
\chi^{2}
,
Reg-Inv
\chi^{2}
, and MAnorm2 inflate FDR; MAP inflates much more severely (exceeding
50\%
) because it ignores residual heterogeneity. The NPMLE-based methods control FDR while gaining substantial power over the
t
-test. Since
K_{A}=K_{B}
, we have that
A_{i}=\tilde{A}_{i}^{\mathrm{MA2}}
, and so MAnorm2 satisfies Assumption
1
. Its lack of FDR control is due to the parametric model.

•

Setting 2 (parametric prior misspecified, trend).
Same as Setting 1 but with the choice
m(\mu_{i})=-4\,\mathrm{logistic}((\mu_{i}-16)/4)+12
. The parametric methods continue to inflate FDR. Among feasible methods,
Joint-NPMLE
attains the highest power.

•

Setting 3 (unbalanced design, parametric prior, trend).
We take
K_{A}=2
,
K_{B}=10
,
G=10\cdot\mathrm{Inv}\chi^{2}_{10}
,
m(\mu_{i})=-6\,\mathrm{logistic}((\mu_{i}-20)/0.15)
,
\mu_{i}\sim\mathrm{N}(20,0.2)
. MAnorm2 and MAP inflate FDR substantially, and for different reasons: MAnorm2’s
\tilde{A}_{i}^{\mathrm{MA2}}
violates orthogonality in Assumption
1
, and MAP’s plug-in
\widehat{\sigma}_{i}^{2}=\widehat{\xi}^{2}(A_{i})
ignores residual heterogeneity.

•

Setting 4 (no residual heterogeneity, external side-information, trend).
We take
K_{A}=3
,
K_{B}=5
,
G=\delta_{1}
,
m
as in Setting 3,
\mu_{i}\sim\mathrm{N}(20,0.2)
, with
M_{i}=\mu_{i}
supplied externally. The trend perfectly determines
\sigma_{i}^{2}
, MAP’s ideal scenario. MAnorm2 and
Joint-NPMLE
do not apply. We see that
Reg-NPMLE
matches the power of MAP and
Oracle
while controlling FDR: the nonparametric residual prior costs essentially nothing, even when not needed.

6
Applications to bulk RNA-seq, ChIP-seq and proteomics

We demonstrate the methodology on three datasets spanning bulk RNA-seq, ChIP-seq, and quantitative proteomics: the melanoma RNA-seq data of
20
, the H3K4me3 ChIP-seq data of
51
, and the A431 proteomics data of
57
. In each case, we work with standard normalized and transformed measurements for which unit-wise linear modeling and the associated Gaussian approximation are routinely used in practice.
4
4

4

For the RNA-seq and ChIP-seq datasets, the
Y_{ij}
are
\log_{2}
-transformed counts, so Gaussianity cannot hold exactly. Treating them as Gaussian is standard practice in
limma
,
limma-trend
, and
voom

(
28
)
; we intend to study the impact of this approximation on downstream inference in future work.
We compare our proposals,
Reg-NPMLE
and
Joint-NPMLE
, against four benchmarks from Section
5
: the
t
-test, Untrended-Inv
\chi^{2}
, Untrended-NPMLE, and
Reg-Inv
\chi^{2}
. We omit MAnorm2 and MAP: MAnorm2’s restriction to two-sample comparisons makes it inapplicable to two of our three datasets, and MAP’s FDR exceeded 30% throughout our Section
5
simulations whenever residual heterogeneity was present. Table
2
reports the number of discoveries per dataset and method.

The mean-variance trends for each dataset are visible in Figure
1
and motivate adjusting for the trend. For each dataset, we also report diagnostic plots assessing the fit of each method’s variance model; these are introduced in detail in Section
6.1
and reused with minor variations in the subsequent subsections.

:
Number of discoveries (BH at target FDR
\alpha
) across the datasets of Section
6
with different methods. Our proposals are
Reg-NPMLE
(Regression-NPMLE) and
Joint-NPMLE
(Joint-NPMLE).

Table
2
shows that the untrended methods make no discoveries at all on this dataset, the t-test makes 2 discoveries, whereas the trended methods make on the order of 100 each. To check whether each method’s variance model fits the data, Figure
2
(a) overlays each method’s model-implied marginal density on a histogram of
\log(S_{i}^{2})
(untrended) or the trend-adjusted
\log(V_{i}^{2})=\log(S_{i}^{2})-\widehat{m}(A_{i})
(trended). This marginal fit (rather than the prior itself, which is latent) is what our convergence guarantees for
Reg-NPMLE
p-values go through (Remark
9
). In Figure
2
(a),
limma
’s parametric Inv-
\chi^{2}
prior fits the untrended empirical marginal of
\log(S_{i}^{2})
poorly. The Untrended-NPMLE prior, in contrast, recovers the empirical marginal closely. After trend adjustment, both
Reg-Inv
\chi^{2}
and
Reg-NPMLE
fit the empirical marginal of
\log(V_{i}^{2})
.

While untrended-NPMLE fits the distribution of
\log(S_{i}^{2})
reasonably well, the main advantage of subtracting the fitted trend is that it produces a markedly tighter empirical marginal:
\log(V_{i}^{2})
is far more concentrated than
\log(S_{i}^{2})
in Figure
2
(a). This is consistent with the strong mean-variance trend in Figure
1
(a) and is the source of the power gain of the trended methods. The corresponding effect on the prior side is visible in Figure
2
(b): the priors on
\tau_{i}^{2}
are visibly tighter than those on
\sigma_{i}^{2}
. Panel (c) displays the estimated bivariate prior
\widehat{H}
which also shows a strong trend.

An additional RNA-seq dataset is analyzed in Supplement
D.3
. There, Untrended-Inv
\chi^{2}
makes the most discoveries despite a clearly misspecified parametric prior (Figure
S3
(a)), casting doubt on those results; after trend adjustment, the parametric assumption becomes reasonable, and
Reg-NPMLE
and
Reg-Inv
\chi^{2}
agree.

6.3
Differential protein expression among miRNA-mimic treatment groups

In this application, we analyze a TMT10-plex–labelled quantitative proteomics dataset on A431 human epidermoid carcinoma cells sourced from
57
. The cells were treated with three different miRNA mimics and compared to a control condition. After peptide-spectrum matching and protein-level filtering, the data are organized as a protein-by-sample abundance matrix, where each entry represents the observed abundance of a protein in a sample. These abundance measurements are continuous-valued and are analyzed on the
\log_{2}
scale, with
n=8,633
proteins and
K=10
samples. The design contains four groups (ctrl, miR191, miR372, miR519) yielding
p=4
covariates, and we test all six pairwise contrasts. Unlike previous settings where the average intensity
A_{i}
is a natural continuous summary, proteomics variance is strongly driven by quantification depth: proteins supported by more identified spectra or peptides tend to have more stable abundance estimates
(
57
)
. Hence, we take
M_{i}
as the number of peptides. Since
M_{i}
is discrete and external,
Joint-NPMLE
from Algorithm
3
does not apply directly; we use the discrete variant from Algorithm
S1
(Supplement
A
), which fits a separate NPMLE within each
M_{i}
bin (binning details in Supplement
D.2
). To unify notation, we still refer to this variant as
Joint-NPMLE
. For all these methods, p-values are computed, and BH correction is applied to control FDR at
\alpha=0.05
.

(b)

(c)

(d)

Figure 4
:
TMT-based quantitative proteomics data of
57
: each
unit
i
corresponds to a distinct protein (
n=8{,}633
) quantified from bulk
TMT reporter-ion intensities, with
K=10
samples,
p=4
, and
M_{i}
equal to
the number of peptides supporting protein
i
. Panels (a) and (b) are

, respectively.
Because
M_{i}
is discrete and external,
Joint-NPMLE
is implemented through the
discrete variant of Algorithm
S1
in the

M_{i}
bin.

.

\sigma^{2}

, one panel per

D.2
).
The priors visibly shift and reshape across bins; evidence of the heterogeneity in
\sigma_{i}^{2}\mid M_{i}
that motivates the discrete variant over
Reg-NPMLE
.

Table
2
shows a consistent ordering across all
six pairwise contrasts: trended methods uniformly outperform their untrended
counterparts, and discrete
Joint-NPMLE
attains the most discoveries throughout.
Unlike the RNA-seq and ChIP-seq applications, here the untrended NPMLE makes
only marginally more discoveries than Untrended-Inv
\chi^{2}
, consistent with
Figure
4
(a), where the parametric Inv
\chi^{2}
prior already
fits the empirical marginal of
\log(S_{i}^{2})
reasonably well. After trend
adjustment, both
Reg-Inv
\chi^{2}
and
Reg-NPMLE
continue to fit the empirical
marginal of
\log(V_{i}^{2})
closely. The corresponding estimated priors are
shown in Figure
4
(b).

To interpret these results, we return to the mean-variance diagnostic in
Figure
1
(c):
S_{i}^{2}
is systematically larger for proteins
quantified from few peptides (small
M_{i}
). This was precisely the concern
of
57
, who worried that methods ignoring the trend might
inflate false positives among low-peptide-count proteins.
Figure
4
(c) supports this concern: the untrended partially
Bayes methods make a disproportionately larger fraction of their discoveries
at small
M_{i}
, a pattern that the trended methods do not exhibit. The
well-specified model in (
16
) does not appear to
hold for this dataset, however: proteins with smaller
M_{i}
exhibit not only a
larger trend but also greater residual variability, as we document in
Figure
S2
of the Supplement. Under this
misspecification, type-I error guarantees for
Reg-NPMLE
follow from
Section
3.2
, but calibration conditional on
M_{i}
is no
longer guaranteed. Discrete
Joint-NPMLE
addresses this by allowing the full
conditional distribution of
\sigma_{i}^{2}\mid M_{i}
to vary across
M_{i}
bins.
Figure
4
(d) shows that the bin-wise estimated priors differ across
M_{i}
levels in both location and shape, indicating
heterogeneity that a single trend with a shared residual prior cannot absorb.
Consistent with this, Figure
S2
in the
Supplement shows that the discrete
Joint-NPMLE
fit aligns more closely with
the data than
Reg-NPMLE
.

7
Frequentist guarantees

We have throughout invoked a partially Bayes specification that treats the primary parameters
\theta_{i}
as deterministic (as in a frequentist analysis), while the nuisance parameters follow an unknown distribution. For instance, for
Joint-NPMLE
, we assumed that

(\mu_{i},\sigma_{i}^{2})\sim H
in (
14
). We close by considering whether our results hold up when all parameters (including the nuisance parameters
\mu_{i},\sigma_{i}^{2}
) are treated as deterministic.
We conduct such a frequentist analysis in Supplement
M
and refer to
22
for more intuition on this setting, building on compound decision theory
(
55
)
. We show that FDR control is robust to the fully frequentist setting, while the conditional interpretation relies on the validity of the partially Bayes specification. More precisely:

•

Asymptotic FDR control (under critically dense signals) continues to hold when p-values are computed according to either Algorithm
2
or Algorithm
3
. However, FDR is controlled at level
\alpha
and not at
\alpha n_{0}/n
. This means that one is not allowed to replace BH by a null-proportion adaptive method such as Storey’s procedure
(
48
)
.

•

Under the partially Bayes framework, our proposed p-values were shown to be asymptotically uniform conditional on
(M_{i},S_{i}^{2})
, or on
V_{i}^{2}:=S_{i}^{2}/\xi^{2}_{\text{mis}}(M_{i})
in the misspecified setting. In the frequentist setting, the p-values do not satisfy such conditional uniformity, nor do they satisfy unconditional uniformity. Instead, they satisfy a weaker condition referred to as asymptotic compound p-values in
24
(also see
1
), that is,
\limsup_{n\to\infty}n^{-1}\sum_{i\in[n]:\theta_{i}=0}\mathbb{P}\big[\widehat{P}_{i}\leq t\big]\leq t
for all
t\in[0,1]
, where
\widehat{P}_{i}
can be either
\widehat{P}_{i}^{\mathsf{tr}}
or
\widehat{P}_{i}^{\mathrm{jt}}
.

Reproducibility.

All results in this paper are fully third-party reproducible with code under the following Github repository:
https://github.com/wyling01/limma-trend-partially-bayes
.
